Codex:"Hermes Desktop Nous est maintenant installé et lancé.

Ce que j’ai trouvé :
- Nous Desktop officiel = intégré à `NousResearch/hermes-agent/apps/desktop`, même config/sessions/skills que CLI et gateway. Docs officielles : [Nous Desktop App](https://hermes-agent.nousresearch.com/docs/user-guide/desktop).
- `fathah/hermes-desktop` = companion externe Electron qui installe/configure Hermes Agent via le script officiel, avec backend local ou distant. Source : [fathah/hermes-desktop](https://github.com/fathah/hermes-desktop).

Cause du crash :
- L’installateur échouait sur `git pull failed` parce que `C:\Users\amado\AppData\Local\hermes\hermes-agent` avait des modifications locales dans le code source Hermes.
- J’ai préservé ces modifs dans un stash Git :
  `codex-preserve-local-hermes-overrides-before-nous-desktop-install-2026-06-03`
- Puis `git pull --ff-only origin main` est passé.

Deuxième blocage corrigé :
- Le script installateur Nous avait un bug Windows local : `Get-Command npm` renvoyait `npm.ps1` + `npm.cmd`, donc PowerShell passait un objet à `&`.
- J’ai patché seulement le script en cache :
  [install-main.ps1](C:/Users/amado/AppData/Local/hermes/bootstrap-cache/install-main.ps1)
- Ensuite le build Desktop a terminé.

État opérationnel :
- Binaire créé :

- Raccourcis créés :

- App lancée, fenêtre `Hermes` active, processus répondants.

- Config runtime toujours correcte : OpenRouter + `z-ai/glm-4.7-flash` + `/api/v1`, sans remettre de patch dur dans le code source.

Et depuis que je suis sur TikTok, YouTube et X, il y a une question qui revient tout le temps, mais vraiment presque tous les mois. J'ai la question "Quel est le meilleur sas à lancer entre le B2B ou le B2C ?" Et en fait cette question c'est une fausse question. La réalité c'est que c'est beaucoup plus nuancé que B2B vs B2C. D'abord, parlons de quelques définitions. Le B2B, business to business, tu es un professionnel qui vend quelque chose à d'autres professionnels. Ensuite, le B2C, c'est business to consumer. Tu es un professionnel qui vend à des particuliers, à des consommateurs. Sauf qu'en fait, ça va beaucoup plus loin que ça. On pourrait se dire, c'est vrai, il y a des particuliers, des professionnels, dont on vend à l'un ou à l'autre. Sauf qu'en fait la réalité c'est qu'un particulier il peut acheter pour lui pour son usage personnel ou aussi pour son usage professionnel ou encore pour un usage avancé comme par exemple les gens qui ont des hobby. Et ça c'est ce qu'on va commencer à appeler du B2 presumer. C'est-à-dire que tu es un professionnel qui vont à des particuliers qui ont un usage poussé, un usage de power user de ce que tu as créé comme SAS ou comme app. Personnellement, au lieu de diviser ça en deux catégories, le B2B et le B2C, j'aime bien le diviser en cinq catégories. Et je vais te montrer ça ici pour que tu comprennes la différence entre toutes ces catégories et que ça t'aide à mieux vendre tAS, à mieux le positionner parce que c'est comme ça que tu comprendras lequel d'entre eux te permettra de faire le plus d'argent. Commençons tout de suite par une chose. Je vais faire les deux grosses catégories. Je vais mettre le B2B bien évidemment et je vais mettre le B2C. Ensuite, on a donc les PME et les grands groupes, donc les autres entreprises classiques. Et je vais donner un exemple très concret. Comme je l'ai dit, moi j'ai un SAS qui s'appelle Tizi et dessus, il y a trois offres et c'est du self service. C'est-à-dire que les gens viennent, s'inscrivent, s'abonnent et cetera. On a du gratuit 49 € par mois et 99 € par mois. Ça c'est le business model de base qui vise des professionnels parce que ce sont des formateurs qui vendent des formations. Donc quasiment tout le monde est un professionnel chez Tizi, sauf rare exception. Et ce qui s'est passé avec Thizi, c'est qu'au bout d'un moment, on a commencé à voir un jour une entreprise qui nous a demandé "Oui, mais moi j'ai besoin qu'on me fasse un bon de commande, un devis et cetera." À partir de là, on a commencé à introduire une nouvelle offre parce qu'on s'est rendu compte qu'on avait en face de nous un interlocuteur qui travaille dans une grosse boîte et qui ont des besoins différents. Ils ont des besoins qui fait qu'il va y avoir beaucoup d'échanges entre nous. Et donc, il fallait qu'on ait une offre plus adaptée que du self service. On a donc créé une offre pour les entreprises et petit à petit, cette offre est devenue Tachy Plus, un site complètement différent basé sur le même logiciel mais avec une nouvelle offre qui n'a aucun rapport qui commence à 4000 € par an. Est-ce que tu vois la différence ? Ici, on avait une offre à 49 € par mois, ce qui veut dire que par an, on est à 490 ou 990, donc 1000 €. Là où ici, on est passé à 3990 4000 €. Pourquoi il y a une telle différence de prix alors que sur le fond, c'est le même logiciel ? parce que on ne s'adresse pas aux mêmes cibles, ni même la même offre. Ici, on a une offre avec plus d'accompagnement, plus de choses et surtout le discours n'est pas du tout le même. Et c'est ça la vraie différence entre un indépendant et une PME, c'est la manière dont tu vas adresser le discours. Donc tu vois bien que c'est pas une question de B2B ou B2C. Tout d'abord, on commence par le particulier classique qui a un besoin personnel ou casual, ce que j'appelle un besoin non récurrent ou en tout cas vraiment très ponctuel. sur ce genre de B2C, c'est là où c'est tu vas voir les plus gros taux de CHN, tu vas avoir les plus grandes difficultés, les plus grosses plaintes parce qu'on est sur du grand public et en général quand on veut faire ça, on veut faire de la masse mais vraiment de la masse. Typiquement pour moi, c'est le genre d'application comme animé AI Face App, ce genre de chos que les gens vont faire une fois, c'est drôle, c'est marrant, c'est à la mode et ils vont ensuite passer à autre chose. Là pour moi, on est dans ce genre de cas. Ça c'est le gros du B2B. C'est des choses où les gens en fait n'y accordent que très peu d'importance ou je dirais plutôt que leur implication, elle sera vraiment très faible et pas forcément sur le long terme. En numéro 2, on a les particuliers qui utilisent quelque chose comme un hobby ou pour atteindre un certain objectif. Là, on est déjà sur des prosumers, des gens qui vont utiliser ton app et ton SAS de manière poussée. Soit parce qu'ils ont un hobby, par exemple, admettons une app autour de ton hobby du basket. Tu es à fond dans le basket, tu as une app que tu utilises très souvent pour laquelle déjà tu es beaucoup plus prêt à payer sur le long terme parce que c'est ton hobby, c'est quelque chose auquel tu tiens, c'est quelque chose qui te tient à cœur et donc tu es prêt à mettre plus d'argent là-dedans ou quelque chose pour atteindre un objectif comme les applications de sport. Les applications de sport, on est déjà sur du prouser aussi parce que c'est un objectif qui va être récurrent, qui va être long terme et où certains sont prêts à mettre plus d'effort parce que c'est pour atteindre un objectif précis sur le long terme. En général, quand tu es dans la classe ici, ton but c'est de faire de la viralité, c'est toucher plein de gens et d'espérer convaincre une petite partie d'entre eux de payer. Donc ici, on fait vraiment de la masse. Dès qu'on rentre là, on a parfois plus de masse que sur du B2B, mais parfois tu vas voir quasiment la même masse que sur du B2B indépendant. parce qu'en fait ici c'est quasiment comme un usage d'un d'indépendant. C'est juste que c'est pas un but professionnel. Et enfin, on arrive dans la dernière catégorie, les gens qui sont des particuliers mais qui utilisent ça pour leur vie professionnelle. Et dedans, j'inclus les investisseurs. Ça ne veut pas dire que c'est une entreprise qui fait des choses à but professionnel. Pas forcément. Et il peut y avoir plein de raisons à ça. Celui que je veux vraiment mettre dans l'aspect pro ici, c'est par exemple les investisseurs. Donc est-ce qu'il y en a un parmi ces cinq qui est meilleur que les autres ? Et ben en fait non parce que ça va dépendre de l'idée. Tous ont des avantages et des inconvénients. Donc cette niche là est dure à convaincre de lâcher le porte-monnaie. Par contre, elle est facile à cibler. Si on prend les PME et les groupes, ils sont plus dures à cibler. ## Ce que dit vraiment la vidéo (le cadre)
Le fondateur (Tizi/Teachizi, SaaS à 2M€) démonte le faux débat **B2B vs B2C**. La vraie segmentation = **5 catégories** :

| # | Segment | Comportement / prix |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | **B2B Indépendants** (solo pros) | Faciles à cibler ; certaines niches refusent de payer (ex. micro-entrepreneurs/facturation) |
| 2 | **B2B PME & grands groupes** | Durs à cibler (prospection), mais **prix bien plus élevés** |
| 3 | **B2C casual/ponctuel** (FaceApp, anime AI) | **Churn maximal**, viralité/masse, one-shot — « relous » |
| 4 | **B2C prosumer** (hobby/objectif : app basket, sport) | Payent plus, récurrent, long terme |
| 5 | **B2C usage pro / investisseurs** (scraper LeBonCoin, bots trading, dashboards) | Prêts à payer, objectif clair |

**Le coup de génie (ton exemple exact)** : *même logiciel*, deux offres :
- Self-service pour indépendants/formateurs → **49-99€/mois** (≈ 490-990€/an)
- Offre entreprise « Teachizi Plus » (même soft, plus d'accompagnement, **discours différent**) → **4000€/an**

→ **8× le prix, même produit.** La leçon : *le prix n'est pas fixé par un instinct de nombre rond — il est fixé par **QUI tu cibles** + **l'offre/le discours**, et par le fait que le besoin soit récurrent/profond ou juste émotionnel.*

## Ce que ça implique pour ton « enlever un zéro » (5000 → 500)
⚠️ **Point critique** : ton offre Grand Slam vise les **Content Forges = agences (≥3 membres, ≥15 projets actifs, 80% de marge en jeu)**. Dans la grille de la vidéo, c'est du **segment 2 (PME)** — exactement le segment où il a mis **4000€/an, pas 490€**.

Donc enlever un zéro pour le **même segment** = sous-pricer un acheteur PME qui saigne des milliers d'€ de marge → tu laisses énormément sur la table (l'erreur que la vidéo dénonce).

**« Enlever un zéro » n'a de sens que si tu changes AUSSI la cible + l'offre** — c.-à-d. créer un **tier self-service bas pour le segment 1/4** (créateurs solo / freelances de contenu / prosumers), comme son offre 49€/mois à côté de l'offre 4000€/an.

## Ta question month / year / signup = c'est LE choix de segment
Les 3 framings ne sont pas interchangeables — chacun **désigne un segment différent** :

| 500 € … | ≈ équivalent | Segment visé | Cohérence |
|---|---|---|---|
| **/mois** | ~6000€/an | **PME/agence** (segment 2) | ✅ proche de ton offre actuelle, juste reframée en mensuel « qui paraît petit » |
| **/an** | ~42€/mois | **Indépendant / prosumer** (segment 1/4) | ✅ mais = NOUVEAU produit self-service, pas l'offre Content Forge agence |
| **à l'inscription** (setup one-shot) | 500€ une fois | **Indépendant low-touch** | ✅ onboarding self-service, puis petit retainer |

## Ma recommandation (Doctor Frame, honnête)
**Ne pas enlever un zéro sur l'offre Content Forge agence** — elle reste premium (segment PME, 4000€+/an, justifié par les 80% de marge sauvée + le SLA + le système propriétaire).

À la place : **créer un 2e tier "self-service Solaris Lite"** à ~**500€ (setup à l'inscription) + petit retainer mensuel**, ciblant le **créateur/freelance de contenu solo (segment 1/4)** — même moteur, offre allégée, discours différent. Exactement la manœuvre Tizi → Teachizi Plus, mais en sens inverse (tu descends une marche, tu ne casses pas le premium).

Ça te donne une **échelle de valeur** (Hormozi : la "value ladder") au lieu d'un seul prix cassé.

Instead of having clouded code look at 500 different decisions, its context window, its memory, its autoco compaction, all of that will lead to less accurate decisions. So when you break apart all the possible decision space into all these separate agents, you have a different way to also trace how a decision is made. And you could probably imagine the example use case of where this would be helpful. Imagine you have 5,000 rs. And instead of shoving those 5,000 resumes through a typical applicant tracking system or one clawed code session where it will inevitably find bias, context window issues, bloating, you basically break apart all those resumes into specific stacks. Do we pick this person or this person and explain why? Applying this, you could have a thousand different items and then you have different rounds. Round one could be assessing all items based on X criteria. Then the next round would be on B criteria. And then we would keep going until we get to the final round. So the one key caveat here is that each round could technically have its own rubric. So it doesn't have to be 7, 10, 50 rounds with the exact same criteria. And a prompt for this could say, "Use a workflow to rank every resume for the back-end engineer role by running a tournament of pair-wise comparisons against a rubric instead of scoring each one cold, where each head-to-head match is its own comparison agent, and the deterministic loop holds the bracket, so only the running order stays in context. So, if you want to steal a lot of my wording here, you're going to be able to access all these prompts in the second link down below. So, don't worry about it for now. But we're basically just setting the tone for this whole bracket to transpire. Now the last pattern is called loop and done. And this is very similar to something like slash goal where all you do is instead of telling the agent go and do x 10 times, you just say don't stop until we reach this specific outcome. So if you have brand new findings for a particular matter, it will keep going and spin up brand new agents to go through double check, triple check until you reach the specific desired result. So maybe you have a bug that occurs in a platform you've put together. Maybe you vibe coded it. Maybe this bug happens once every 30 times, but you can't usually spot it. You have to keep refreshing. You have to keep trying different combinations to reproduce the bug. You could theoretically say go through and run this until you receive the bug and then once you get the bug try and trace what's happening and how we can resolve it. And if we need another application, imagine you send this loop and done on a wild goose chase to go through all of your conversations, your JSON L files that represent your cloud code sessions. And you say, "Keep looking through every single one of these conversations for every pattern I've made until you have a comprehensive, non-duplicative list of every single thing that I could improve to be that much better at cloud code." So you could say something like build a workflow that hunts down a flaky test that fails maybe one in 50 runs. Keep forming theories about the cause in adversarially testing each one in its own isolated work tree. And when it comes to the prompt, it could look something like this. Build a workflow that hunts down what is called a flaky test in the test folder that fails maybe one in 50 runs. keep forming theories about the cause and adversarily testing each one in its own isolated work tree. This is basically a separate session for an agent looping and spawning new attempts with no fixed pass count. So basically telling it we are not telling you to go check this 10 times or 20 times. Keep checking it until this specific result is achieved. Now those were these six core patterns but this is how you go from just knowing about them to actually becoming dangerous with them which is stacking them on top of each other. So imagine you have a realistic scenario where you vibe coded some CRM and this CRM has an onboarding flow for clients and you want to find ways to improve that onboarding flow. Make it very thoughtful and think of different order consequences. Maybe you go and say fan out a series of agents to look at the codebase and pull out all the insights on what should change and why. And then once that is pulled out then you move on to pattern number three which was the adversarial verify step. So now it has a series of devil's advocates that goes against all the findings that were found. Then from that step, maybe you add on a loop until done. So maybe you combine a slash goal in the scenario and then you apply that to this specific circumstance. So maybe you have a process that keeps going until it can't find any more optimizations that it could possibly make given the specific avatar who's using the platform. The best part about this is that you don't have to design it by hand. It's really a matter of just using the keywords in the right way to get the confirmed result or the ideal result. And if you wanted to put all of this into one single prompt, you could say something like, "Build a workflow that audits every file under codebase, fans out one agent, keyword right here, fans out per file, has a separate agent try to refute each of the findings against the code." And this is our adversarial verify and loops until a clean pass turns up with no new issues. Return only the confirmed issues each with the file and the exact line. Then for good measure, you could add slash goal do not stop until a full clean pass finds no new issues. Now, do you need this last sentence? Maybe not. It would get the idea. But if you want to add it for an additional layer, then you can absolutely do so. Now, like I said before, you can also share workflows very similar to how you share skills. And pretty much every workflow is purely a JavaScript file. And when you combine this JavaScript file with your skill file and maybe any additional markdown file that it depends on, all you'll get is one overall folder with the skillmd, the JavaScript file, and anything else that's involved. So it will look something like this. If we go into this folder, you'll have this rubric, you'll have the skillmd, and then you'll have this verify claims workflow JavaScript file. And if you're not familiar, if you ever want to save a workflow, all you'd have to do is go into, let's say we run this. So we do slashworkflows. ## Verdict : **D09 d'abord.** Mais pas D09 *seul*.

### Pourquoi D09 gagne (3 raisons doctrinales)
1. **Séquence de la valeur** : leads → vente → livraison. Sans lead, il n'y a **rien à livrer**. Aujourd'hui la landing ne capture **aucun** prospect (les CTA n'ont pas d'endpoint). Structurer plus de SOP de livraison, c'est **huiler une machine qui n'a encore rien à produire**.
2. **Théorie des Contraintes** (ton propre framework de réinvestissement Dan Martell + Finance) : on investit le capital sur **LA contrainte**. La contrainte n'est pas la livraison (zéro deal à livrer) — c'est **l'acquisition** (on ne capte même pas un lead).
3. **Validation avant construction** (Sales **S9 pre-sell** / Million Dollar Weekend) : on valide la demande avec un **engagement payant** *avant* de bâtir la capacité de livraison. Sur-spécifier les SOP maintenant = optimisation prématurée (E-Myth / YAGNI).

### Le « mais » (pour ne pas violer ta propre garde-fou)
Ton **VOC guardrail** dit : *aucune acquisition ne se lance sans ICP, VOC, promesse, canal, métrique **ET Ops handoff***. Et l'offre **promet un SLA** (2-rounds, −80%). Donc avant de **scaler** l'acquisition, Ops doit fournir **UN minimum** : le **"Ops launch gate"** = la preuve qu'on peut livrer le **cœur** de la promesse (Asset-First **Couche 0+1** : versioning + pré-validation sous SLA 2-rounds). 

→ Ce n'est **pas** un buildout SOP complet. C'est **un seul gate bordé**. Le reste des SOP se **durcit ensuite, avec les données de livraison réelles** (People PE21 : itérer depuis le réel, pas spéculer).

## Donc la séquence juste
```
1. D09 — API lead-capture (IT)         → débloque Growth (capter le 1er lead)
2. Ops launch-gate minimal (Couche 0+1) → garantit qu'on peut livrer le cœur promis
3. Growth acquisition (Content Forges)  → premiers leads
4. Sales Doctor Frame → 1er deal
5. Ops SOP hardening ← piloté par la livraison réelle des 1ers clients
```
**On ne fait PAS plus de SOP maintenant** — on ferait du sur-mesure pour des clients qui n'existent pas encore.

## Mon offre concrète
J'attaque **D09** maintenant (API route Next.js dans `solaris-aaas` : form → store + tracking, qui résout aussi le "source of truth/tracking" du VOC). Et je borde le **Ops launch-gate** en 1 fiche courte (pas un SOP complet).

3- MON COMBAT MON HISTOIRE - ÉPISODE 1 :" For those who don't know me, the people close to me call me Abou. But I'm also known by the name Abou Debeing, artist and producer. And more recently, Abou Kamara is the man who made the decision to step inside the cage. Against all odds. But very few people know why. Very few truly understand me, and even fewer accept it. Far from the training sessions of 2013 with Black M and Vincent Parisi. OK, alright, the one and only Black M, eyes wide open, here with my guy Abou Debeing for an intense training session. Before that, I was part of Sexion d'Assaut from around 2006, 7, 8, 9, 10, 11, 12. My name is Barack Adama, I was part of Sexion d'Assaut back in the day. Part of the Wati B label, back in the day. Today, I've launched my own label, H24. I discovered rap very young. It was probably through my my father, directly, who watched a lot of music videos on TV. I came across not necessarily rap at first, but Jackson's music videos, the Fugees, for those who know them. For the older ones who know, the Fugees. Rap, as you know. I discovered rap it was I was in middle school, Year 7, and I discovered rap through Doc Gynéco. A friend of mine named Alioune. He's the one who played it for us and that's how I got into it. Then there were people from my neighbourhood who were rapping too, like 9bless, Lio Pétrodollars was rapping. And that's how I gradually discovered the world of rap. It got me hooked. You know. Rue Milton, that's where a lot of things started, because that's where Sexion d'Assaut would gather to freestyle. That's my street, full of diversity. My street is the street I genuinely grew up on. And yeah, let's say that's where everything started, because it was kind of our headquarters, it was the headquarters. It was often at my place or at Maska's place. That's where all our guys would come, and my guys came from all over the capital. They didn't all come from our neighbourhood. Some came from the 18th, some came from the 3rd, the 10th, some from Bois-l'Abbé. People came from everywhere. The Sexion d'Assaut mindset was all about being number one across all of Paris, across all of Paris, even all of France. We built it up step by step. We wanted to be the best. First all of North Paris, then all of South Paris, then East, West, and then it was a snowball effect. Once we started doing it, it was a conquest. Once we had conquered one place, then we wanted the next. And then... It was Game of Thrones, Sexion d'Assaut style. We wanted all the lands, every single one. The Sexion d'Assaut mindset was all about performance, yeah. And pushing past your limits. The fact that, you know, there were a lot of us,
so there was competition between us. A very healthy competition. Meaning we would meet up every Saturday, and every Saturday, everyone had to bring a new verse from the week. You know, one, two or three, depending on the most active ones. And each person would spend the week preparing their verse for Saturday. We'd hold freestyle sessions like that and everyone would drop new material. You know. Lyrical delivery, performance was one of the things that Sexion d'Assaut put front and centre. It was something we loved to push because we were raised on American freestyle, where rappers were dead serious, not joking around at all, just facing the camera straight on. Sometimes there wasn't even a beat. It was purely about showing your flow. How you start, how you how you command the flow, how you rhyme, how your delivery and even your body language all mattered. That's what we called performance. Us. Sexion d'Assaut. Spitting borough by borough. I'd say the inevitable Lino, because after all, they had Secteur A. Then there was Youssoupha, the Bantu lyricism, he's an incredibly strong lyricist too. He hit us hard. The first time we heard a Youssoupha track, I was on my computer. Adama from Sexion. He was on his own computer at home and we both hear the track "Eternel Recommencement." We all called each other. Have you heard this guy? He's as good, maybe even better than Lefa. Because we were comparing the two. They had roughly the same writing style. Have you heard how he does that thing where he bounces back, it's insane. I'd heard of them. Sexion d'Assaut, because they started getting on my radar. I love using that line. It's simple what he said in a song. I've always loved that line. He said, rappers don't listen to each other, they watch each other. So in reality, you think you're the best until you hear someone who's likely stronger than you. And at some point, Sexion d'Assaut, when they started making noise, like many people, I discovered a lot through them. That was around the tecktonik era. I thought, we have a problem here, we have a problem. Anyone who says Sexion d'Assaut isn't hip-hop simply doesn't know hip-hop. To me, that's the energy. THE CHAIRMAN, host of the show OUI HUSTLE, the show that brings people together and motivates. Because, you know, from an outsider's perspective, coming from the suburbs myself, I no longer saw Parisian rappers freestyling and all that. In it, there was Jarod, Inso, John K, Le H, Dr Beriz, DIMS, LAS. But every person who came out of L'Institut always took a hit and was always driven by Sexion d'Assaut. I was born in Paris, 15th arrondissement. I spent my whole life, let's say, more or less within Paris itself, between the 9th arrondissement. What people call the chic ghettos. It was a place that wasn't really destined for us. It's a neighbourhood, let's say a bit bourgeois, where there came an arrival of young people from immigrant backgrounds, and on the other side, Belleville, a neighbourhood far more working class, a neighbourhood that brings together in one area, at the Belleville metro station, four arrondissements, the 19th, 20th, 10th and 11th. I grew up in a neighbourhood that was hybrid. Why? Because in truth, it's ranked among the wealthier neighbourhoods. But inside, there were perhaps the poorest people in all of Paris too. It was hybrid, meaning the poor people who lived in that neighbourhood, they were poor, you know, they were in squats, or in tiny rooms, or in small apartments, or crammed in with entire families in one or two-room places. You know. In Belleville, I lived at 164 Rue Saint-Maur, a completely run-down building. But I also lived on Rue Robert-Houdin, which is, let's say the Lorient neighbourhood. It's just behind Rue Saint-Maur, and that's where I cut my teeth. And in the 9th, I lived at 13 Rue Rodier, Rue Rodier, which sat right behind Rue Milton. That famous Rue Milton that over the years would become very important for us. Then, my first primary school was on Avenue Parmentier. Then when my father moved to the 9th, and my mother was going through her situation, they took me back, retrieved me let's say, from child services, and because of that separation, I had to take the metro every morning to get to Anvers, I was doing Belleville to Anvers, in Year 2 at primary school. Imagine a seven-year-old kid with a schoolbag bigger than his own head, going back and forth on line two with all the craziness of that era. It was wild, it was just wild. I was independent, probably too early. That was it. I'm an only child of my mother and father, but I have brothers and sisters on both sides. And I stayed close to my cousins, both on my mother's side and my father's side. My name is Kamara Safique, but people call me Shaf, and I am the first cousin of Abou Debeing. My family connection to Abou: he's my cousin. My name is Kamara Abou. His name is also Kamara Abou. One day, I was at home, very young, and he came over with his father. So my uncle, his father, had bought him a toy gun. Back then, we were obsessed with those. I saw it and the moment I saw him, I was already happy, and then on top of that, the gun, I thought wow! And what stuck with me the most is that right at that moment, Abou said to his father, Dad, where is Safique's gun? And I watched as they brought out my gun and gave it to me, and right then, that moment was something I'll never forget. You know, every time I think about him, I think of that moment. Like I said, he's always been with us. It was much later that I understood he was my cousin, but for me he was my brother. We weren't even allowed to call each other cousins. Because we were all always at S.Pri's place, loads of us, writing away, papers everywhere. I remember we'd pull all-nighters. He would show up coming from the Eminem school. 50 Cent. I was more from the Dipset school. And then, where we had common ground, was artists like Lil Wayne, artists like that. You could feel that he was growing up in a school that elevated them, but also put pressure on them. Even if the talent was always there, someone like Dadju, even early on, they always knew how to rap. Besides, it's always funny when I listen back to Dadju rapping, it's tough, it's really tough, it's you've got to respect it, honestly. Real respect. Abou Tall and Dadju are passionate people. They're people who genuinely love the culture. Dadju sang, Dadju sings, Dadju knows how to rap too. I remember a documentary I saw a few years ago now. Time flies, where Youssoupha was crowning him. Dadju, I met him during a period when, for me, I was at university. He too was at university. We crossed paths at the Wati boutique, and H Magnum was watching us and said, you rap well, you sing well, you two should form a group. And it happened just like that, as simply as that. Debeing, it must be fifteen years now, fifteen years, fifteen years that we've known each other well. We even knew each other a little bit before that. But we've known each other well, let's say, for fifteen solid years, when I was in a group with Shinsekai, etc. He was part of L'Institut. L'Institut were the younger generation of Sexion d'Assaut, and he had that lyrical delivery thing. When we knew there was a Skyrock freestyle battle, we'd all gather, we'd train on all kinds of different beats. You had to be capable of rapping over any beat and adapting your verse written at a certain BPM to any instrumental. S.Pri. He was all about pure assonance. The Dipset vibe, New York style and all. Debeing was, pure lyrical delivery with near-perfect assonance every time. And I was far more versatile. I was more of a Dirty South head, so I had a flow a bit more fluid, different, less choppy than theirs. Everyone brings what you'd call their own techniques, like in Naruto, in One Piece, and you had to cut through. So he's a pure lyricist at heart. He's L'Institut, he has a sharp pen, he writes exceptionally well. To this day, he's always said I need to keep grinding, I need to get on more collaborations, do one-on-ones, 50 bars, 50 bars, etc. It's a generation at a crossroads, they came along just after us. There's Mac Tyer, Nessbeal for sure, there were some serious ones in there, and them, they came into a generation a bit more stylish, a bit more fashion-forward, and all that, but raised by rappers, by rap music. I wanted to leave a mark. It wasn't just me who had that mindset, GIMS etc. We all had the same mindset, we wanted to leave a mark so we also wanted a next generation to carry that mark forward. You know, we had a mindset that was a bit particular. There is one thing I didn't know back then, which is that when I was doing my freestyles, I would be in the exact same state of mind as when I was getting ready for sparring sessions or heading to training. The discipline, the determination, the repetition, again and again and again, to be able to excel on the day. And I didn't know I was going to become addicted to that feeling. I think we had a plan, we had a plan. Abou and I talked a lot, a lot, a lot, a lot, a lot, for hours walking through Paris, you know, from one end of the East to the West, from North to South. You know, S.Pri, he's the one with whom, back in 2008 when we had nothing, I have this memory. We're walking in the street, you know the things you do when you're young, you walk from I don't know, from Porte de Bagnolet all the way to the Champs-Elysees, asking ourselves questions. Through S.Pri, I meet a woman. From my HND, I move to a degree, and in that degree, I meet well, there are girls in my class, and Debeing, he's my guy, I'd often invite him to my parties and everything. But not just that. My mother would often go to Senegal and all, so he was often at my place while I was in class, making himself comfortable. That's how it was, so I'd find him there in the morning, the evening, the next day, the day after that, with this particular woman. And at some point, things got tense. We'd fall out, get back together, fall out again, get back together. And one day she calls me because she couldn't reach him, and she says to me, yeah, I can't get hold of your friend. Listen, either we meet up, maybe in the same world as him, or we meet up, or I tell you right now on the phone, I have some news to share with you. So I'm thinking, no, no, no, I wasn't there, I mean I wasn't there in their private life, why am I caught up in all of this? Why am I the one holding the ring? And I said to her, yeah but so, she says no, no, no, no, no, you're the one who's going to tell him. So I'm lying there, asleep, I hear a knock. I'm banging on the door because I'm trying to call him, he was sleeping. I'm hammering on the door. He comes over, he breaks the door down, I'm under the covers, I open up. What's going on? Yeah what's up? You're crazy. So we're laughing, well I'm laughing, but him, he's under stress. And suddenly it comes out. I say to him, man, I'm not going to beat around the bush. Here it is. There's a child on the way, a child on the way. There's a baby coming, she's pregnant. A baby coming? She's pregnant? I have nothing to say but that's not possible. And you, who told you what? I don't understand any of this. And then there it was. After the birth of my son. I'm telling you straight. Everything kind of turned upside down in my head. It's 2010. Sexion d'Assaut is breaking through, they want to put us on the map, but at the same time, I'm a young father. There are my flaws, some of my street demons coming back, and I hadn't. I hadn't necessarily made the right choices. It wasn't my first sentence. And I didn't know if it was going to be the last one at that point. But that sentence changed me profoundly. I'm telling you straight, I wasn't the same person anymore, and I know that there were many people who were worried about me. When he got out, I couldn't understand it, I couldn't. I had the feeling of seeing, of not recognizing my friend at all. What was he doing in prison? There are dark sides we had never seen. But how could Abou end up in prison? When he got out of prison in 2013, he came to live with me because, well, he had his personal issues. By then, he had been through a lot, so much, so many disappointments, so many battles. And if he takes the path I can see coming, I won't be able to follow him."

4- MON HISTOIRE MON COMBAT - ÉPISODE 2:"For those who don't know me, the people close to me call me Abou. But I'm also known by the name Abou Debeing, artist and producer. And more recently, Abou Kamara the man who made the decision to step inside the cage. Against all odds. But very few people know why. Very few truly understand me, and even fewer accept it. But when it comes down to it who really knows me? Where we come from, they shape you from the very beginning. I was taught I was taught and forged by legends. Telling our story, telling our story that is my fight. And I think back then, his state of mind was he went through, he went through several stages. There's one stage that, for me personally, really left a mark the prison stage. It was his prison, because he was someone I was hanging out with practically every day. I felt for him. Honestly, I felt for him. I felt for myself too because he was my guy, you know. We talked a lot, like I told you, and I hurt for him because I was genuinely afraid this would be a turning point in his life because he was walking a tightrope. He was right on the edge, really right on the edge, between as they say in Star Wars, the dark side of the Force and the light side. It's a dark side he never showed openly, in front of us. He really kept that hidden he was very private. You know, it was later on that I saw Debeing's little vices his weaknesses, the drinking, things like that. But to me, my brother Debeing has always been the quiet, respectful little brother that's really who he was, you know, so he gave you nothing to worry about. Nothing to be concerned about. With Abou, I always had the feeling that he was hiding his street side from me. When I say street, I mean his trouble in the streets I had no idea. Because whenever I saw Abou, he was always smiling, always kind. "Black, come on, let's freestyle" he had this little lisp, he was just adorable, honestly. So in my mind, I just couldn't picture that side of him that he could end up doing time, you know, going to prison. When I found out he was locked up, going back and forth, in and out of prison I was more shocked than anything else. I thought how can Abou end up in prison? Turns out he had another side to him. Prison can give you something just as easily as it takes from you. In my case, it gave me something. It allowed me to truly know myself. For real. How many people truly know themselves? I had the time to discover who I was, to get acquainted with myself. There were hard moments, yes, but nobody sent me there. When he got out, I couldn't understand it, I couldn't. I had the feeling of seeing, of not recognizing my friend actually, he was, he was colder. I could see it in his eyes, in everything all of it, I could see that it was different. And right then I feared for my friend. Because I told myself okay, I don't know which direction he's heading in, and if he takes the one I can see coming, I won't be able to follow him. I won't be able to follow him. I did a sentence at 17 two months. Then another sentence at 20 six months. Then in 2013, when I got out after two years I was a different man. In those two years, I had picked up a weapon and sharpened my skills. And I felt ready to create something. Artistically speaking. L'Institut had changed. They had lost a lot of members. And when Debeing and Ahmidou came back out, they brought something different with them right away. Something heavier, more structured he was stronger, ten times stronger, genuinely. Sharper rhymes, a clearer voice, well-crafted lyrical structures. We all agreed this was it. He was ready. Originally, within L'Institut, he wasn't the one who stood out the most. But there was a before and an after his last sentence. And we all thought wow. He had taken a real leap ahead of everyone. He knows his craft inside out. He plays with the sounds, the sonorities inverted rhymes, ping-ponging back and forth. And he knows that once he's milked that technique, he needs to shift to a new flow to finish his technical phrase. That's what a passionate artist looks like he's actually someone, like people in my circle, in the sense that as a rapper, he's a student of rap you can tell he approaches it academically, and he's a man of L'Institut at heart. I'm certain that lines like those, he drew inspiration from me. I'm sure of it. And that lisp of his came through again in that one. There are real punchlines in there. There are things in there that sometimes you can't even say anymore. You know, we're living in a time now where I feel like we're a bit too censored. And right there I truly recognize the Debeing who wrote those lyrics in prison. He had leveled up compared to the Debeing he was before prison. For real. I truly believe that this prison sentence, brother it changed his life in its own way. Ten years and I hadn't seen that. That's Debeing right there. Precise. That's precise. It hits hard and it says something. And I remember that freestyle, we still talk about it. All the time. It's a freestyle that's a genuine classic today. He held his own in that one. Debeing. I took it personally because he and I share a history that runs incredibly deep. If our groups were football teams with positions on the field, he'd be a midfielder, for real. And a midfielder isn't someone who shines but he's not someone who goes unnoticed either. He's someone absolutely essential. L'Institut was a real group. I remember the first time I heard them they completely blew my mind. For me, they represented the raw, unfiltered side of Sexion d'Assaut. You know. Honestly, I loved L'Institut more than Sexion d'Assaut that's my group. If I'm being completely honest they're my favourite rap group, but people don't know that because they never got the opportunity to sustain their career. And even what they did before being signed to Wati B people never saw it. That whole generation Fababy, Still Fresh, incredible lyricists people, when they hear Mielleux today, I wonder if they realize that L'Institut and all those artists were bridging two worlds. You hear the instrumental, we tear it apart, we feed off each other and then when there are girls around, we turn on the charm too. Something I've already had the chance to say before my generation, when all is said and done, was incredibly strong lyrically, but maybe we were prisoners of rap itself. We played by the rules but we didn't reinvent the rules enough. And the next generation they knew how to reinvent them. That's why there were careers, moments of connection, and far more sustained success. In 2014, we released a track one of the very first Afro tracks to break out of the urban scene. Billet Facile, on which I was an artist featuring The Shin Sekai, Dr Bériz, and DRY. With Debeing, we always connected on pretty much everything culturally, musically. We listen to the same things whether it's Afro music. Afro music really broke through around, around 2018, let's say 2018 something like that. That's when people really started listening to Afro. But we were already deep into it since, since about 2013. 2012, we were already putting out tracks Afro tracks and everything. Me personally, for example, when I was in the group with Shinsekai and the others, we were always sharing Afro sounds, like that we were listening to a lot of P-Square. And from there, we even made a track for a Wati B compilation called Billets Facile. It was one of the very first Afro tracks that really took off at that time. And that was before I went solo and before all of that came about. Before everything. When I used to see him, I was just watching and laughing like, what is Debeing doing now? Oh, he's going into Afro now? He's doing Afro, he's doing this and that oh, he's buzzing. You go to a club, you'd hear Debeing Debeing was playing in every single club. It cracked me up, because it wasn't what anyone had planned, you know. He was supposed to yeah, he was going to do rap, you know, he was it wasn't planned, but he knew how to adapt and also at that moment, something that nobody talks about is that, let's say, this was one of the first major turning points in French rap the arrival of the showcase circuit, club anthems in nightclubs whereas before, French rap wasn't that. It didn't play in clubs it was only American rap that got played in clubs. But around that time, everything shifted. When Vegedream, MHD arrived, Dadju, Debeing when that whole wave, Naza, Keblack when that whole wave hit, everything changed overnight. And he, just like that, as always bounced back, opened up, seized the right opportunities, understood the moment, reinvented himself. That's Debeing for you. And when I arrive at the point where I come out, the track goes to you. Something happens. We reach the moment where Abou Debeing truly comes into his own because this is a track in the image of Paris, a celebration of Paris one that the PSG players would make their own. I knew Serge Aurier and I asked him if he could make a video for a track I was about to drop. He sends back a video. In the video, there's Serge Aurier, Kurzawa, Rabiot, and Matuidi. He tells me wait, don't post the video yet. If tomorrow we win, I'll post the video and I'll say it's the new PSG anthem. 75th minute a goal for Paris Saint-Germain, the third goal of the season for the international. He posts it. And from that moment on, you could say things really began for Abou Debeing. I'm not someone who throws himself compliments, but I am someone who is very self-aware and very pragmatic. One plus one equals two. So if people are consuming your music, it means you've done something right. If people aren't consuming your music, it means maybe you haven't. Or maybe you have done something good, but you haven't managed to reach them. So when I see that I've had a string of hits well, I'm proud of that. Together with my team, my manager at the time, Joss Dadju, Franglish, who was part of our team at that point, and even S.Pri Noir, Abou Tall people I not only made music with, but also grew up alongside. We're proud of what's happening for our circle, for our team. Because each of us moves forward at our own pace, at our own level. I was genuinely proud of that, and I still am. We always got along well, and then, when when I went solo, me too we were able to build our own connection, just the two of us, and since we got along so well, it showed in the music. Right off the bat, we listen to the same things so we naturally fall into that, we share our references our shared references being R. Kelly and Usher, with the sounds of Singuila searching for that feeling, chasing the same woman. And I followed that up with a track featuring an artist who would go on to become an artist of international renown someone very few people knew back then. But I had the instinct. That would be Aya Nakamura. We made our collaboration together Sorry which we released in January 2016. I see you. That's a classic today. Whenever I perform it whether it's at a concert or a showcase his part I put the mic down. Everyone sings along. No, it's a great moment. I was on an insane run myself. And so we kind of found each other again it had been a while since we'd collaborated, and it was actually him who came in with the chorus idea. He whispers it to me. Then he starts writing. We finish the chorus together, we had the remnants of that mindset you have to deliver, which means that even if it was melodic and all that, you still had to hit hard. And him I remember he was in a Vybz Kartel mode when he recorded it. I remember it clearly. When it came out, it was an absolute smash. To this day. Even though it came out a long time ago. I feel like when I perform it, I feel like it came out yesterday. That's when you see that all those years of grinding, of writing in the dark, of passion for the music in the end, they lead to moments like these. That's not it. We also had Brazilian influences we were listening to Brazilian music too. On tour with me. It's just we simply made our dreams come true. For real we did. Like I told you, we connect deeply with Debeing we're on the same wavelength on so many things. We watched Michael Jackson concerts together, we watched Bruno Mars concerts, Justin Timberlake, and so on. And our goal was to put on the greatest shows. So when I take the stage, Debeing absolutely wants me if I'm doing a show, it has to be the best show out there better than every other artist. And we did it. You know, in the end, I got to a point where I knew I was putting on the best shows. Because we kept pushing ourselves. And sometimes we'd put on incredible shows and still not be satisfied. We weren't satisfied because at that point, I messed up on that part. At that moment, that thing we were extremely demanding and very hard on ourselves on what we wanted, and we always raised the bar higher. And when it was a festival, it was even worse because at a festival, you've got multiple artists on the bill, so there are many artists and we were even more demanding. It was absolutely essential to be the best show. And when people watch the show and they say oh wow those two go all in. For television and everything. What are we doing here, Abou? We are completely focused, and we've just seen the stage for the first time. It's massive. So together with Julien Dadju does the the debrief, they exchange ideas, they take care of everything you don't see. But it's heavy it's heavy. Right now I feel the pressure. I'm dead serious I've got no room to laugh. Right now, I'm it's massive, it's huge. I've rarely seen this. You're not performing with Dadju but whenever you can, you're there, supporting him on every date, giving advice everything. What is your actual role, day to day, in Dadju's story? I'm not sure just to be there, to be present, honestly. My role is to be there without being there. I've always been in his corner. I believe that every idea Dadju has I've pushed him to see every idea through and I mean fully whether it's moving a pen, going to Rennes, staging the concert a certain way, dropping the right video clip, putting together the right choreography. Let's say I'm a man who works in the shadows. There's Joss who manages, Saïd, Hicham but me, I'm right behind him, you know trying to bring him everything I possibly can. Debeing is someone who really pushed us toward sport. Sports combat sports specifically. We did that with Debeing, Badji, Papis and that was our thing, our routine. We started with boxing, did a bit of wrestling after, then eventually moved into MMA. But that's it, it was that drive to stay fit, to keep the cardio up, to stay in shape. That came from Debeing. So my name is Badji Mohamed, to introduce myself properly. Security guard for quite a few artists. The first time we trained together and Debeing was with us we put the gloves on, we warmed up properly, and we got started gloves on, right from the jump. And the moment this guy gets into his stance, throws his shot tucking his chin in his neck, raising his guard, getting set and I immediately saw his guard was airtight. Right away I said wait, what are you how are you pulling that off? Have you boxed before? He goes no, never. He had the attitude of someone who'd been at it for years. Badji who would become a pillar in my life. And Bams Bams, who was Dawala's older brother. The fact that they worked as bodyguards they'd worked with so many artists that their experience is unlike anyone else's. And they're street guys, just like me you know, and those are people who would push me in a very particular way Badji especially, toward combat sports. The fights and scraps we've had there have been plenty, plenty of them. And the thing is, I don't even know where it comes from. People who think that because someone picks up a microphone, sings, they're not capable of hitting you in the face and leaving you sitting there thinking why did I do that? And unfortunately Debeing is the perfect example he'll go on stage, he'll sing, he'll put on a show you challenge him to a one-on-one on the side he'll take his shirt off, he'll settle in and tell you let's go, nice and easy and he'll follow through. I met him through Dadju, and since he was often with Abou, especially back then, when he still lived in France we were all based in Paris. So I met Abou, and then it just happened naturally. You know how it goes you meet a friend of a friend, and next thing you know, he's your friend too. That's how it was. Tony Yoka a friend. Being an Olympic champion it's something, it's a unique experience, and the people who go through it face storms, face criticism. I hope that he manages to silence all those critics. What he's done very few could do it. And in his category in boxing, punches land. They land hard. So it's only natural to go through highs and lows. He can still do great things again and again. For me, it's it's like like when it's as if I said I make music he smiles a little, you know, and when he tells me he was boxing it made me smile too, but I could tell he genuinely knew the sport in the sense that he had a real sporting culture boxing culture specifically. He knew fighters, he knew this and that we'd talk about certain techniques, and he knew them inside out. But but always, let's say, at a casual level from time to time, just to let off steam, to get a feel for it. But never seriously, not at all not professionally, and certainly not with any real commitment. You know. September 2025 I get a call from my little sister. I have to leave immediately for Thailand. My best friend Haroun decides to come with me. And when I get there, what I see hits me like a wall. Despite everything, I don't let go of training I don't let go, I don't let go and that's what keeps me standing. My mother is somewhere between life and death. Sometimes in life you go through highs and lows. And when you hit the lows, you need to retreat into yourself. For me I know that when I've had my own defeats, I don't want to talk to anyone and with Abou specifically, he was one of the only people I actually wanted to be around at those times, because he never crowded my mind, and we could just live day by day in those moments. I think you need to disconnect and Abou needed to disconnect, because he needed, I think, to step back and focus on himself to be with his mother, to be with his family. You know. It marked him deeply inevitably because on top of everything, I know how much he loves his mother and at that moment, he needed he needed Haroun and he was by Haroun's side constantly. Let's call it what it is. He was shattered. Seeing your mother lying in a hospital bed not knowing if she'll live or die. That's devastating. When I found out that my mother was ill in a way, I always knew that one day I would have to make a choice."
